# **Architecture**

With the immigration of [northern Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_and_Scandinavian_Americans) into Wisconsin and the [upper Midwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Midwest), they brought the techniques of building [log homes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Log_house) with them.[\[173\]](#page-25-18)

The [Milwaukee Art Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Art_Museum) is known for its Quadracci Pavilion created by Spanish architect [Santiago Calatrava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_Calatrava). The Quadracci Pavilion contains a movable, wing-like Burke *[brise soleil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brise_soleil)* that opens up for a wingspan of 217 feet (66 m) during the day, folding over the tall, arched structure at night or during inclement weather. [\[174\]](#page-25-19)

[Frank Lloyd Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Lloyd_Wright), an architect known as the pioneer of [Prairie School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prairie_School) architecture and the [Usonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usonia) home concept, was raised in Wisconsin. Wright's home and studio in the 20th century was at [Taliesin,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliesin_(studio)) south of [Spring Green, Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring_Green,_Wisconsin). Taliesin and the Usonian [Jacobs I House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_and_Katherine_Jacobs_First_House) in Madison are listed as [UNESCO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO) [World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Site) as part of "[The 20th-Century Architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_20th-Century_Architecture_of_Frank_Lloyd_Wright) [of Frank Lloyd Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_20th-Century_Architecture_of_Frank_Lloyd_Wright)".[\[24\]](#page-19-9) Other notable works of Wright in Wisconsin include the

## ![](_page_12_Picture_11.jpeg)

[Taliesin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliesin_(studio)) was the studio of architect [Frank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Lloyd_Wright) Lloyd [Wright.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Lloyd_Wright)

[Annunciation Greek Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annunciation_Greek_Orthodox_Church) in Wauwatosa, [First Unitarian Society of Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Unitarian_Society_of_Madison), and [Johnson Wax Headquarters.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnson_Wax_Headquarters) [Monona Terrace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monona_Terrace) in Madison, a convention center designed by Taliesin architect Anthony Puttnam, is based on a 1930s design by Wright.[\[175\]](#page-25-20)

# **Alcohol**

Drinking has long been considered a significant part of Wisconsin culture, and the state ranks at or near the top of national measures of per-capita alcohol consumption, consumption of alcohol per state, and proportion of drinkers. Consumption per-capita per-event, however, ranks low among the nation; number of events (number of times alcohol is involved) is significantly higher or highest, but consumption at each event smaller, marking Wisconsin's consumption as frequent and moderate.[\[176\]](#page-25-21) Factors such as cultural identification with the state's heritage of German immigration, the long-standing presence of major breweries in Milwaukee, and a cold climate are often associated with the prevalence of drinking in Wisconsin.

## ![](_page_12_Picture_16.jpeg)

The Miller [Brewery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miller_Brewing_Company) in Milwaukee

Many large breweries were founded in Wisconsin, largely in Milwaukee, which gained the epithet "Brew City" before the turn of the century. [Miller Brewing Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miller_Brewing_Company), [Pabst Brewing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pabst_Brewing_Company)

[Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pabst_Brewing_Company), [Joseph Schlitz Brewing Company,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Schlitz_Brewing_Company) and [Jacob Leinenkugel Brewing Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Leinenkugel_Brewing_Company) all began as local businesses before entering national markets. Several other popular craft brews include Ale Asylum,[\[177\]](#page-25-22) [Capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_Brewery), [Sprecher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sprecher_Brewery), and [New Glarus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Glarus_Brewing_Company), the latter being well known for the Spotted Cow Farmhouse Ale only sold in Wisconsin.[\[178\]](#page-25-23)

In Wisconsin, the legal drinking age is 21, except when accompanied by a parent, guardian, or spouse who is at least 21 years old. Age requirements are waived for possessing alcohol when employed by a brewer, brewpub, wholesaler, or producer of alcohol fuel. The minimum legal age to purchase alcohol is 21, with no exceptions.[\[179\]](#page-25-24) The Absolute Sobriety law states that any person not of legal drinking age (currently 21) may not drive after consuming alcohol.[\[180\]](#page-26-0) DUI offenses were lowered to BAC 0.08 in 2003 as a result of federal government pressure.[\[181\]](#page-26-1)

# **Cuisine**

Wisconsin's cuisine is famous for [its cheese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_cheese) as well as other dairy products. [Colby cheese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colby_cheese) was first created in Wisconsin in 1885 (named after [Colby, Wisconsin\)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colby,_Wisconsin), and [brick cheese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brick_cheese) was first created in the state in 1877.[\[182\]](#page-26-2)[\[183\]](#page-26-3) The state is the only producer of [Limburger cheese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limburger_cheese) in the United States. [Cheese curds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheese_curd) are a popular variant that can be eaten separately cold as a snack, or covered in batter and fried as an appetizer, often served with [ranch dressing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranch_dressing) as a dipping sauce. [Hot and spicy cheese bread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_and_spicy_cheese_bread) is a popular type of bread created and sold in Madison.

Wisconsin is the nation's top producer of [cheese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheese), the state's official dairy product. It is also a leading producer of [cranberries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cranberries), the state's official fruit, and [ginseng.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ginseng) [\[184\]](#page-26-4) [Dane County Farmers'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dane_County_Farmers%27_Market) [Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dane_County_Farmers%27_Market) in Madison is the nation's largest producers-only farmer's market.[\[185\]](#page-26-5)[\[186\]](#page-26-6)

## ![](_page_13_Picture_3.jpeg)

Fried [cheese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheese_curd) curds

The Friday night [fish fry,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_fry) often battered and fried [perch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perch) or [walleye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walleye), is traditional throughout Wisconsin, while in northeast Wisconsin and [Door County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Door_County,_Wisconsin) the [fish boil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_boil) is more popular. The [supper club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supper_club) is another common phenomenon of Wisconsin culinary heritage and often a destination for fish frys; other fried food are common side dishes, along with condiments of tartar sauce and [cole slaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cole_slaw) or crimson slaw, a variety of cole slaw that incorporates Wisconsin cranberries.[\[187\]](#page-26-7) [Beer cheese soup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_soup) is usually made from a variety of beer and cheddar or Colby cheese with sausage, potatoes, and green onions.

[Booyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Booyah_(stew)) is a [stew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stew) common to Wisconsin, commonly using meat and a mirepoix of vegetables cooked together in a "booyah kettle" over low heat for several days.[\[188\]](#page-26-8)

The southeastern city of [Racine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racine,_Wisconsin) is known for its [kringle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kringle), a sweet flaky pastry often served as a dessert. The recipe was brought by [Danish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danish_Americans) [immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danish_Americans) to the region in the 1800s and became the official state pastry of Wisconsin in 2013.[\[189\]](#page-26-9)[\[190\]](#page-26-10) The [Wisconsin State Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_State_Fair) is known for its giant [cream puffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cream_puff). [\[191\]](#page-26-11)[\[192\]](#page-26-12)

The [butter burger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butter_burger) originated in Wisconsin, most likely in [Solly's Grille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solly%27s_Grille) in [Glendale.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glendale,_Wisconsin) [\[193\]](#page-26-13) [Culver's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culver%27s) is a midwestern [fast casual food restaurant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast_casual_restaurant) [chain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast_casual_restaurant) originally from [Sauk City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauk_City,_Wisconsin) and currently headquartered in [Prairie du Sac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prairie_du_Sac,_Wisconsin) known for serving butter burgers, fried cheese curds, and frozen custard. [La Croix Sparkling Water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Croix_Sparkling_Water) originates from [La Crosse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Crosse,_Wisconsin).

## **Music**

Milwaukee hosts [Summerfest,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summerfest) dubbed "The World's Largest Music Festival", every year[\[194\]](#page-26-14)[\[195\]](#page-26-15) The festival is held at the lakefront [Henry Maier Festival Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Maier_Festival_Park) just south of downtown, as are a summer-long array of [ethnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic) musical festivals. The [Wisconsin Area Music Industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Area_Music_Industry) hosts an annual awards show for top Wisconsin artists.[\[196\]](#page-26-16) The [Mile of Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mile_of_Music) in [Appleton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appleton,_Wisconsin) is an annual all-original music festival of varying styles. It focuses on undiscovered touring musicians and bands.[\[197\]](#page-26-17) Other music festivals include [Hoofbeat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoofbeat_(festival)) country fest, Country Jam USA, the [Hodag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hodag_Country_Festival) [Country Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hodag_Country_Festival), [Lifest,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lifest) Porterfield Country Music Festival, Country Thunder USA in Twin Lakes,[\[198\]](#page-26-18) and Country USA.

## ![](_page_13_Picture_11.jpeg)

[Summerfest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summerfest) is an annual music festival in downtown Milwaukee.

## **Recreation**

The varied landscape of Wisconsin makes the state a popular vacation destination for outdoor recreation. Winter events include skiing, ice fishing and [snowmobile derbies.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Championship_Snowmobile_Derby) Wisconsin is situated on two Great Lakes and has many inland lakes of varied size; the state contains 11,188 square miles (28,980 km2 ) of water, more than all but three other states[—Alaska,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska) Michigan, and Florida.[\[199\]](#page-26-19) The [Wisconsin Shipwreck Coast National Marine Sanctuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Shipwreck_Coast_National_Marine_Sanctuary) was established in 2021 in the waters of Lake Michigan off Wisconsin and is the site of a large number of historically significant [shipwrecks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shipwreck). [\[200\]](#page-26-20)[\[201\]](#page-26-21)[\[202\]](#page-26-22)

Outdoor activities are popular in Wisconsin, especially hunting and fishing. One of the most prevalent game animals is the [whitetail deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitetail_deer). Each year in Wisconsin, well over 600,000 deer-hunting licenses are sold.[\[203\]](#page-27-0) In 2008, the [Wisconsin Department of Natural Resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Department_of_Natural_Resources) projected the pre-hunt deer population to be between 1.5 and 1.7 million.

## **Sports**

Wisconsin is represented by major league teams in three sports: football, baseball, and basketball. [Lambeau Field,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambeau_Field) located in [Green Bay,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Bay,_Wisconsin) [Wisconsin,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Bay,_Wisconsin) is home to the [National Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Football_League)'s [Green Bay Packers.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Bay_Packers) The Packers have been part of the NFL since the league's second season in 1921 and are the smallest city franchise in the NFL, and the only one owned by shareholders statewide.[\[204\]](#page-27-1) The [Milwaukee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Brewers) [Brewers,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Brewers) the state's only major league baseball team, have played in [American Family Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Family_Field) in Milwaukee since 2001. Before the Brewers, Milwaukee had two prior Major League teams. The first team, also called the Brewers, played only one season in 1901 before becoming the [St. Louis Browns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Louis_Browns). Milwaukee was also the home of the now[-Atlanta Braves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_Braves) franchise when they moved from Boston from 1953 to 1965, winning the [World Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Series) in 1957.[\[205\]](#page-27-2) The [Milwaukee Bucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Bucks) of the [National Basketball Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Basketball_Association) play home games at the [Fiserv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiserv_Forum) [Forum.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiserv_Forum) [\[206\]](#page-27-3)

The state also has minor league teams in hockey [\(Milwaukee Admirals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Admirals)) and baseball (the [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Timber_Rattlers) [Timber Rattlers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Timber_Rattlers), based in [Appleton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appleton,_Wisconsin) and the [Beloit Sky Carp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beloit_Sky_Carp) of the [High-A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-A) minor leagues). In addition to these affiliated minor league teams, Wisconsin has two [American Association of Professional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Association_of_Professional_Baseball) [Baseball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Association_of_Professional_Baseball) teams, being the [Milwaukee Milkmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Milkmen) based in [Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin,_Milwaukee_County,_Wisconsin), [\[207\]](#page-27-4) and the [Lake Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Country_Dock Hounds) [Dock Hounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Country_Dock Hounds) based in [Oconomowoc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oconomowoc,_Wisconsin). [\[208\]](#page-27-5) Wisconsin is also home to nine [Northwoods League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwoods_League) teams. The [Madison Mallards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison_Mallards), the [La Crosse Loggers,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Crosse_Loggers) the [Lakeshore Chinooks,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakeshore_Chinooks) the [Eau Claire Express,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eau_Claire_Express) the [Fond du Lac Dock Spiders,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fond_du_Lac_Dock_Spiders) the [Green Bay Rockers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Bay_Rockers), the [Kenosha Kingfish,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenosha_Kingfish) the [Wausau Woodchucks,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wausau_Woodchucks) and the [Wisconsin Rapids Rafters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Rapids_Rafters) all play in a collegiate all-star summer league. In addition to the Packers, Green Bay is also the home to an [indoor football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indoor_American_football) team, the [Green Bay Blizzard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Bay_Blizzard) of the [IFL.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indoor_Football_League) The state is home to the seven-time MISL/MASL Champion [Milwaukee Wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Wave). [\[209\]](#page-27-6) Wisconsin is also home to [Forward Madison FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forward_Madison_FC), which is a professional soccer team that plays in the USL [League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USL_League_One) [One.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USL_League_One) The Northern Elite Football League consists of many amateur semi-pro teams from Wisconsin.

Wisconsin also has many [college sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_sports) programs, including the NCAA [Division I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I) [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Badgers) [Badgers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Badgers), [Milwaukee Panthers,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Panthers) and [Green Bay Phoenix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Bay_Phoenix). The [Marquette Golden Eagles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marquette_Golden_Eagles) of the [Big East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_East_Conference_(1979%E2%80%932013)) [Conference,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_East_Conference_(1979%E2%80%932013)) the state's other major collegiate program, is known for its [men's basketball team.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marquette_Golden_Eagles_men%27s_basketball) Many other schools in the University of Wisconsin system compete in the [Wisconsin Intercollegiate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Intercollegiate_Athletic_Conference) [Athletic Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Intercollegiate_Athletic_Conference) at the [Division III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_III_(NCAA)) level. The conference is one of the most successful in the nation, claiming 107 NCAA national championships in 15 different sports as of March 30, 2015.[\[210\]](#page-27-7)

The [Milwaukee Mile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Mile), an oval track opened in 1903, is the oldest operating motorsports venue in the world, having hosted the [Indy Car Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indy Car_Series) and [NASCAR Xfinity Series.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASCAR_Xfinity_Series) [\[211\]](#page-27-8) [Road America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Road_America) near [Elkhart Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elkhart_Lake,_Wisconsin) hosts races in the [Indy Car Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indy Car_Series), IMSA [Sports Car Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IMSA_Sports Car_Championship), [Sports Car Club of](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_Car_Club_of_America)

[Lambeau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambeau_Field) Field is home to the publicly owned Green Bay [Packers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Bay_Packers) of the [National](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Football_League) Football League.

## ![](_page_14_Picture_5.jpeg)

Road [America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Road_America), near [Elkhart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elkhart_Lake,_Wisconsin) Lake, has hosted [motorsport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorsport) races since the 1950s.

[America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_Car_Club_of_America) GT [World Challenge America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GT_World_Challenge_America) and [Trans-Am Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Am_Series) and the [Moto America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moto America) Superbike Championship as of 2024. The [World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Championship_Snowmobile_Derby) [Championship Snowmobile Derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Championship_Snowmobile_Derby) is held at [Eagle River, Wisconsin.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eagle_River,_Wisconsin) The world championship [off-road racing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Off-road_racing) event is held at [Crandon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crandon_International_Off-Road_Raceway) [International Off-Road Raceway.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crandon_International_Off-Road_Raceway)

Wisconsin is home to the nation's oldest operating [velodrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Velodrome) in [Kenosha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenosha) where races have been held every year since 1927.[\[212\]](#page-27-9)

[Sheboygan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheboygan,_Wisconsin) is home to [Whistling Straits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whistling_Straits) golf club which has hosted PGA [Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PGA_Championship) in 2004, 2010 and 2015 and the [Ryder Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryder_Cup) golf competition between USA and Europe in 2020.[\[213\]](#page-27-10) The [Greater Milwaukee Open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Milwaukee_Open), later named the [U. S. Bank Championship in Milwaukee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U. S._Bank_Championship_in_Milwaukee), was a PGA Tour tournament from 1968 to 2009 held annually in [Brown Deer.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_Deer) In 2017, [Erin Hills,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erin_Hills) a golf course in [Erin, Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erin,_Wisconsin), approximately 30 miles northwest of Milwaukee, hosted the [U. S. Open](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U. S._Open_(golf)). [\[214\]](#page-27-11)

# **Government and politics**

The [Constitution of Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Wisconsin) outlines the structure and function of state government, which is organized into three branches: executive, legislative, and judicial. The [Wisconsin Blue Book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Blue_Book) is the primary published reference about the government and politics of the state. Re-published every two years, copies are available from state legislators.[\[215\]](#page-27-12) The law of the [Menominee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menominee) also applies within the [Menominee Indian Reservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menominee_Indian_Reservation).

## **State government**

The executive branch is headed by the [Governor of Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Wisconsin). The current governor, [Tony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Evers) [Evers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Evers), assumed office on January 7, 2019. The Wisconsin Constitution grants the governor a veto on bills passed by the state legislature as well as a [line-item veto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Line-item_veto) on appropriation bills. A [lieutenant governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_Governor_of_Wisconsin) succeeds the governor in the event of any removal from office and performs any duties assigned by the governor. The current lieutenant governor is [Sara Rodriguez.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sara_Rodriguez) The other elected constitutional offices in the executive branch are the [secretary of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_of_Wisconsin) [\(Sarah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Godlewski) [Godlewski\)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Godlewski), [treasurer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Treasurer_of_Wisconsin) [\(John Leiber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Leiber)), [attorney general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney_General_of_Wisconsin) ([Josh Kaul\)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josh_Kaul), and the non-partisan [superintendent of public instruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superintendent_of_Public_Instruction_of_Wisconsin) [\(Jill Underly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jill_Underly)).[\[216\]](#page-27-13)[\[217\]](#page-27-14)

## ![](_page_14_Picture_14.jpeg)

The [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_State_Capitol) State Capitol is located on the [isthmus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isthmus) between Lake [Mendota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Mendota) and Lake [Monona,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Monona) in the city of [Madison.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison,_Wisconsin)

The [Wisconsin State Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_State_Legislature) is Wisconsin's legislative branch. The Legislature is a [bicameral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameral) body consisting of the [Wisconsin State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_State_Assembly) [Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_State_Assembly) and the [Wisconsin State Senate.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_State_Senate) The Assembly has 99 members, and the Senate has 33 members. All 99 members of the Wisconsin Assembly are elected in a two-year term cycle without [term limits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Term_limits_in_the_United_States). Similarly, all 33 members of the Wisconsin Senate are elected in a four-year cycle, also without term limits.[\[218\]](#page-27-15) Half of the Senate is elected every two years. Members of both houses of the Legislature vote within their ranks to select presiding officers, such as the [Speaker of the Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_(politics)) and the [President of the Senate.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Senate) Legislators in both the Senate and the Assembly receive an annual salary of \$55,141. Over two years, each legislator is allotted \$66,008 to cover general office expenses, printing, postage and district mailings.[\[219\]](#page-27-16)

Wisconsin's court system has four levels: municipal courts, circuit courts, the Court of Appeals, and the Supreme Court. Municipal courts typically handle cases involving [local ordinance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_ordinance) matters. The [circuit courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Circuit_Court) are Wisconsin's [trial courts,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trial_courts) they have [original jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Original_jurisdiction) in all civil and criminal cases within the state. Challenges to circuit court rulings are heard by the [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Court_of_Appeals) [Court of Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Court_of_Appeals), consisting of sixteen judges who typically sit in three-judge panels. As the state's highest appellate court, the [Wisconsin Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Supreme_Court) may hear both appeals from lower courts and original actions. In addition to deciding cases, the Supreme Court is responsible for administering the state's court system and regulating the practice of law in Wisconsin.[\[220\]](#page-27-17)

## ![](_page_15_Picture_1.jpeg)

The Senate [Chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_State_Senate) of the Wisconsin State Capitol

# **Federal representation**

Following the 2020 census [reapportionment,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_congressional_apportionment) Wisconsin has eight seats in the U. S. House of Representatives. As of the [119th United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/119th_United_States_Congress), six federal representatives are Republicans while two are Democrats. [Gwen Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwen_Moore) is the most senior member of the Wisconsin delegation to the U. S. House of Representatives, while the least senior is [Tony Wied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Wied). Wisconsin's [senior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seniority_in_the_United_States_Senate) [U. S. senator,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senator) [Ron Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Johnson), is a Republican, while its junior, [Tammy Baldwin,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tammy_Baldwin) is a Democrat.[\[221\]](#page-27-18)

Wisconsin is under the [appellate jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appellate_jurisdiction) of the [United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Seventh_Circuit), which includes district courts for the [Eastern District of Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_District_Court_for_the_Eastern_District_of_Wisconsin) and [Western District of Wisconsin.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_District_Court_for_the_Western_District_of_Wisconsin)

## **Politics**

### **2024 United States presidential election in Wisconsin**

## ![](_page_15_Figure_8.jpeg)

Wisconsin is considered a [swing state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swing_state) at the federal level, being won by either the [Democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)) or [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States)) nominees.[\[222\]](#page-27-19) Republican [Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump) won the state by 0.87% in the [2024 presidential election;](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_States_presidential_election_in_Wisconsin) it had the closest margin of any state in that election. Democrat [Joe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden) [Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden) won the state by a narrow margin of 0.63% in [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Wisconsin); [\[223\]](#page-27-20) Trump won Wisconsin in [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election_in_Wisconsin) by a similarly narrow margin of 0.77%, the first time the state voted for a Republican presidential nominee since [1984.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_United_States_presidential_election) Wisconsin was part of the [blue wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_wall_(U. S._politics)), a group of states the Democratic Party won in each presidential election from [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_presidential_election) to [2012.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_United_States_presidential_election) [\[224\]](#page-27-21) Since achieving statehood in 1848, Wisconsin has been won by Republican presidential candidates 26 times, Democrats 18 times, and once by the [Progressive Party.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_Party_(United_States,_1924%E2%80%931934))

At the statewide level, Wisconsin is competitive, with control regularly alternating between the two parties. Following the [2014 general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Wisconsin_elections) [elections,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Wisconsin_elections) the governor, lieutenant governor, attorney general, and treasurer were all Republicans, while the secretary of state was a Democrat.[\[225\]](#page-27-22) However, in [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Wisconsin_elections), Democrats won all constitutional statewide offices on the ballot, the first time this happened in Wisconsin since 1982.[\[226\]](#page-28-0) [Among Wisconsin's 46 governors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_governors_of_Wisconsin), 32 were Republicans, 12 were Democrats, one was a [Whig,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whig_Party_(United_States)) and two were [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Progressive_Party) [Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Progressive_Party) members.

In a 2020 study, Wisconsin was ranked as the 25th easiest state for citizens to vote in.[\[227\]](#page-28-1) Some have argued the state has experienced [democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding) since 2011.[\[228\]](#page-28-2)[\[229\]](#page-28-3)[\[230\]](#page-28-4) Some political scientists classify Wisconsin as a [hybrid regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_regime); [\[228\]](#page-28-2) the state's [House of](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives) [Representative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives) and [legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_legislature_(United_States)) elections are considered to be [free but not fair,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_and_fair_election) with districts undergoing "extreme [partisan gerrymanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerrymandering)" to entrench Republicans "beyond [electoral rotation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_the_United_States)".[\[228\]](#page-28-2)[\[229\]](#page-28-3)[\[231\]](#page-28-5) The Wisconsin Supreme Court overturned the legislative gerrymander in the 2023 ruling of *[Clarke v. Wisconsin Elections Commission.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarke_v._Wisconsin_Elections_Commission)* [\[232\]](#page-28-6)

During the [American Civil War,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) Wisconsin was a [historically Republican state;](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_United_States_Republican_Party) in fact, it is the state that gave birth to the Republican Party, although ethno-religious issues in the late 19th century caused a brief split in the coalition. The [Bennett Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bennett_Law) campaign of 1890 controversially required English as the sole medium of instruction in all schools, and Germans switched to the Democratic Party because the Republican Party supported the law. [\[234\]](#page-28-7)

Wisconsin's political history is broad in scope, encompassing [Robert La Follette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_La_Follette) and the [Progressive movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_movement) to prominent anti-communist [Joseph Mc Carthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Mc Carthy). From the early 20th century, the [Socialist Party of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_of_America) had a base in Milwaukee. The phenomenon was referred to as "[sewer socialism"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sewer_socialism) because the elected officials were more concerned with public works and reform than with revolution (although

## ![](_page_16_Picture_0.jpeg)

The 2011 [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Wisconsin_Act_10) Act 10 led to large protests around the state capitol building in Madison.

revolutionary socialism existed in the city as well). Its influence faded in the late 1950s largely because of the [red scare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Red_Scare) and racial tensions.[\[235\]](#page-28-10) The first socialist mayor of a large city in the United States was [Emil Seidel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emil_Seidel), elected mayor of Milwaukee in 1910; the city elected three more socialist mayors in [Daniel Hoan,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Hoan) [Frank P. Zeidler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_P._Zeidler), and [Henry Maier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Maier). Socialist [newspaper editor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newspaper_editor) [Victor Berger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Berger) was repeatedly elected as a U. S. Representative.

Through the first half of the 20th century, Wisconsin's politics were dominated by Robert La Follette and his sons, originally of the Republican Party and later of the [Wisconsin Progressive Party.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Progressive_Party) Since 1945, the state has maintained a close balance between Republicans and Democrats.[\[237\]](#page-28-11) Wisconsin Congressman [Paul Ryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Ryan) was the Republican vice-presidential nominee in the [2012 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_United_States_presidential_election), alongside [Mitt Romney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitt_Romney), [\[238\]](#page-28-12) and later served as 54th [speaker of the House of Representatives.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives) [\[239\]](#page-28-13)

In [1942](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1942_Wisconsin_gubernatorial_election), Orland [Loomis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orland_Steen_Loomis) would be the last Wisconsin [Progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Progressive_Party) elected governor of Wisconsin but would die shortly after being elected. [\[236\]](#page-28-9)

## **International relations**

Wisconsin has sister-state relationships with [Hesse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesse) in Germany, [Chiba Prefecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiba_Prefecture) in Japan, [Jalisco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jalisco) in Mexico, [Heilongjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heilongjiang) in China, and [Nicaragua.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicaragua) [\[240\]](#page-28-14) A [Mexican consulate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_consulate) opened in Milwaukee on July 1, 2016.[\[241\]](#page-28-15)

# **Education**

Wisconsin, along with Michigan and Minnesota, was among the [Midwestern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern) leaders in the emergent American state university movement following the [American Civil War.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) By the start of the 20th century, education in the state advocated the ["Wisconsin Idea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Idea)", which emphasized service to the people of the state. The "Wisconsin Idea" exemplified the Progressive movement within colleges and universities at the time.[\[242\]](#page-28-16)

The [Wisconsin Department of Public Instruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Department_of_Public_Instruction) is the [state education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_education_agency) and public library management agency in the state.[\[243\]](#page-28-17) The department is led by the [State Superintendent of Public](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superintendent_of_Public_Instruction_of_Wisconsin) [Instruction,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superintendent_of_Public_Instruction_of_Wisconsin) a non-partisan, constitutional officer elected every four years in the spring primary, six months after the previous year's presidential election.

Public [post-secondary education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-secondary_education) in Wisconsin consists of two organizations. The [University of](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Wisconsin_System) [Wisconsin System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Wisconsin_System) comprises the state's two tier 1 [research universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_universities) as classified by the [Carnegie Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Foundation_for_the_Advancement_of_Teaching): the flagship [University of Wisconsin–Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Wisconsin%E2%80%93Madison), and the [University of](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Wisconsin%E2%80%93Milwaukee)

## ![](_page_16_Picture_13.jpeg)

The University of [Wisconsin–Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Wisconsin%E2%80%93Madison) is the flagship campus of the [University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Wisconsin_System) of [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Wisconsin_System) System.

[Wisconsin–Milwaukee.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Wisconsin%E2%80%93Milwaukee) [\[244\]](#page-28-18) The system has eleven other comprehensive universities and ten two-year branch campuses. It is one of the largest public higher education systems in the country, enrolling more than 160,000 [students](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_student) each year and employing approximately 41,000 faculty and staff statewide.[\[245\]](#page-28-19) The 16-campus [Wisconsin Technical College System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Technical_College_System) awards two-year associate degrees, one- and two-year technical diplomas, and short-term technical diplomas and certificates. It also provides training and technical assistance to Wisconsin's business and industry community.

[Carroll University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carroll_University) was Wisconsin's first institution of higher education. It was chartered by the territorial legislature on January 31, 1846, and temporarily suspended operations during the Civil War. [\[246\]](#page-28-20) [Beloit College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beloit_College) is the state's oldest continuously operated college, chartered on February 2, 1846.[\[247\]](#page-28-21) [Marquette University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marquette_University) is a tier 2 research university and Wisconsin's largest private university by enrollment.[\[248\]](#page-28-22)[\[249\]](#page-28-23) It became the world's first coeducational Catholic university in 1909.[\[250\]](#page-29-0) Other private colleges and universities include [Alverno College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alverno_College), [Carthage College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carthage_College), [Concordia University Wisconsin,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concordia_University_Wisconsin) [Edgewood University,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgewood_University) [Lakeland University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakeland_University), [Lawrence University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_University), [Medical College of Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_College_of_Wisconsin), [Milwaukee School of Engineering,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_School_of_Engineering) [Ripon College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ripon_College_(Wisconsin)), [St. Norbert College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Norbert_College), [Wisconsin Lutheran College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Lutheran_College), and [Viterbo University.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viterbo_University)

# **Transportation**

Wisconsin is served by eight commercial service airports, in addition to a number of [general aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_aviation) airports. [Milwaukee Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Mitchell_International_Airport) [International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Mitchell_International_Airport) is the largest airport located in Wisconsin, [categorized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FAA_airport_categories) as a medium-hub primary commercial service facility. [\[251\]](#page-29-1) [Dane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dane_County_Regional_Airport) [County Regional Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dane_County_Regional_Airport) and [Appleton International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appleton_International_Airport) are classified as small-hub primary commercial airports that service the Madison and Fox Cities areas.

Wisconsin is served by multiple [intercity bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercity_bus) operators, which provide service to 71 stops and 53 cities. The following carriers provide scheduled bus service: [Amtrak Thruway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak_Thruway), [Badger Bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badger_Bus), [Flixbus,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flixbus) [Greyhound Lines,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greyhound_Lines) [Indian Trails](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Trails), [Jefferson Lines,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jefferson_Lines) [Lamers Bus Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lamers_Bus_Lines&action=edit&redlink=1), [Megabus,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megabus_(North_America)) [Van Galder Bus Company,](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Van_Galder_Bus_Company) and [Wisconsin Coach Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Coach_Lines). [\[252\]](#page-29-2)

Wisconsin is served by eight Interstate Highways, consisting of five primary routes and three auxiliary routes. The first part of this system was constructed in 1956, and its most recent expansion took place in 2015, with the addition of I-41 to the system. Wisconsin's longest Interstate Highway is I-94. There are also fourteen United States Numbered Highways in the state of Wisconsin, which were designated beginning in 1926 and ending in the mid-1930s. There are also several business routes, usually maintained by local governments. The Wisconsin Department of Transportation maintains 158 state trunk highways, ranging from two-lane rural roads to limited-access freeways. These highways are paid for by the state's Transportation Fund, which is considered unique among state highway funds because it is kept entirely separate from the general fund, therefore, revenues received from transportation services are required to be used on transportation. The majority of state highway funding comes from gas taxes and vehicle registration fees. [253]

## ![](_page_17_Picture_1.jpeg)

## Wind Point Lighthouse on Lake Michigan

Amtrak provides daily passenger rail service between Milwaukee and Chicago through the *Hiawatha*. The *Borealis* provides daily service to Chicago and Saint Paul, Minnesota, and is supplemental to the long-distance cross-country *Empire Builder*, both with stops in several cities across Wisconsin. [254] Commuter rail provider Metra's Union Pacific North (UP-N) line has its northern terminus in Kenosha, the only Metra line and station in the state of Wisconsin. [255] The Hop, a modern streetcar system in Milwaukee, began service in 2018. The 2.1 mile (3.4 km) initial line runs from Milwaukee Intermodal Station to Burns Commons.

# State symbols

Wisconsin is traditionally known as the "Badger State" due to its early history in lead mining. Many of the state's first settlers were drawn by the prospect of mining in southwest Wisconsin, a mineral-rich region which had been contested between Native Americans and the U. S. Some of the miners lived burrowed within hillsides either due to lack of time or finances to build above-ground structures during the winter. Similar to the American badger using its claws to dig holes, the miners were nicknamed "badgers". The University of Wisconsin–Madison adopted the badger as a mascot in 1889 after the nickname; the badger was named Wisconsin's state animal in 1957. [256]

- State nicknames: Badger State, America's Dairyland
- State motto: Forward
- State song: "On, Wisconsin!"
- State bird: American robin
- State animal: American badger
- State domestic animal: dairy cow
- State wildlife animal: white-tailed deer
- State fish: muskellunge
- State insect: Western honey bee
- State dog: American Water Spaniel
- State dairy product: cheese
- State fruit: cranberry
- State grain: corn
- State pastry: kringle
- State beverage: milk
- State cocktail: brandy old fashioned
- State dance: polka
State fossil: trilobite
State flower: wood violet
State tree: sugar maple
- State rock: granite
- State mineral: galena
- State quarter: US coin issued in 2004 featuring a banner with the state motto, the head of a cow, a round of cheese, and an ear of corn.
- State soil: Antigo
